<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the Christian saint. </tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"/><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar" title="Template:Eastern Orthodox Church sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar" title="Template talk:Eastern Orthodox Church sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>John Chrysostom</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;k&#39; in &#39;kind&#39;">k</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="&#39;s&#39; in &#39;sigh&#39;">s</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="&#39;s&#39; in &#39;sigh&#39;">s</span><span title="&#39;t&#39; in &#39;tie&#39;">t</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="&#39;m&#39; in &#39;my&#39;">m</span></span>,<span class="wrap"> </span><span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;k&#39; in &#39;kind&#39;">k</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;s&#39; in &#39;sigh&#39;">s</span><span title="/ɒ/: &#39;o&#39; in &#39;body&#39;">ɒ</span><span title="&#39;s&#39; in &#39;sigh&#39;">s</span><span title="&#39;t&#39; in &#39;tie&#39;">t</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="&#39;m&#39; in &#39;my&#39;">m</span></span>/</a></span></span>; <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span lang="el">Ἰωάννης ὁ Χρυσόστομος</span>; <i><a href="/wiki/Ge%CA%BDez" title="Geʽez">Ge'ez</a></i><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> :ቅዱስ ዮሐንስ አፈወርቅ <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;347</span>&#160;&#8211;&#32;14 September 407)<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> was an important <a href="/wiki/Early_Church_Father" class="mw-redirect" title="Early Church Father">Early Church Father</a> who served as <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Constantinople" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Constantinople">archbishop of Constantinople</a>. He is known for his <a href="/wiki/Homilies" class="mw-redirect" title="Homilies">preaching</a> and <a href="/wiki/Public_speaking" title="Public speaking">public speaking</a>, his denunciation of <a href="/wiki/Abuse_of_authority" class="mw-redirect" title="Abuse of authority">abuse of authority</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilken2013_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilken2013-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> by both ecclesiastical and political leaders, his <i><a href="/wiki/Divine_Liturgy_of_Saint_John_Chrysostom" class="mw-redirect" title="Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom">Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom</a></i>, and his <a href="/wiki/Ascetic" class="mw-redirect" title="Ascetic">ascetic</a> sensibilities. The epithet <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Χρυσόστομος</span></span> (<i>Chrysostomos</i>, anglicized as Chrysostom) means "golden-mouthed" in Greek and denotes his celebrated eloquence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaur1910_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaur1910-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Chrysostom was among the most prolific authors in the early Christian Church, although both <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen of Alexandria</a><sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Hippo" title="Augustine of Hippo">Augustine of Hippo</a><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> exceeded Chrysostom.
</p><p>He is honoured as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Oriental Orthodox Church">Oriental Orthodox</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox</a>, <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Anglican" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglican">Anglican</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lutheran" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran">Lutheran</a> churches, as well as in some others. The Eastern Orthodox, together with the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite" title="Byzantine Rite">Byzantine</a> <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_Churches" title="Eastern Catholic Churches">Catholics</a>, hold him in special regard as one of the <a href="/wiki/Three_Holy_Hierarchs" title="Three Holy Hierarchs">Three Holy Hierarchs</a> (alongside <a href="/wiki/Basil_the_Great" class="mw-redirect" title="Basil the Great">Basil the Great</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nazianzus" title="Gregory of Nazianzus">Gregory of Nazianzus</a>). The feast days of John Chrysostom in the Eastern Orthodox Church are 14 September, 13 November and 27 January. In the Roman Catholic Church he is recognized as a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a>. Because the date of his death is occupied by the feast of the <a href="/wiki/Exaltation_of_the_Holy_Cross" class="mw-redirect" title="Exaltation of the Holy Cross">Exaltation of the Holy Cross</a> (14 September), the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a> celebrates him since 1970 on the previous day, 13 September; from the 13th century to 1969 it did so on 27 January, the anniversary of the <a href="/wiki/Translation_(relic)" title="Translation (relic)">translation</a> of his body to Constantinople.<sup id="cite_ref-romanum_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-romanum-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Of other <a href="/wiki/Western_Christianity" title="Western Christianity">Western</a> churches, including Anglican provinces and Lutheran churches, some commemorate him on 13 September, others on 27 January. John Chrysostom is honored on the calendars of the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Church of England</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> on 13 September.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria">Coptic Church</a> also recognizes him as a saint (with feast days on 16 <a href="/wiki/Thout" title="Thout">Thout</a> and 17 <a href="/wiki/Month_of_Hathor" class="mw-redirect" title="Month of Hathor">Hathor</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biography">Biography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Biography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>John was born in <a href="/wiki/Antioch" title="Antioch">Antioch</a> in 347.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Different scholars describe his mother Anthusa as a <a href="/wiki/Paganism" title="Paganism">pagan</a> or as a Christian.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> His father was a high-ranking military officer. John's father died soon after his birth and he was raised by his mother. He was <a href="/wiki/Baptism" title="Baptism">baptised</a> in 368 or 373 and <a href="/wiki/Tonsure" title="Tonsure">tonsured</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Reader_(liturgy)" title="Reader (liturgy)">reader</a> (one of the <a href="/wiki/Minor_orders" title="Minor orders">minor orders</a> of the church). It is sometimes said that he was bitten by a snake when he was ten years old, leading to him getting an infection from the bite.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a result of his mother's influential connections in the city, John began his education under the pagan teacher <a href="/wiki/Libanius" title="Libanius">Libanius</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> From Libanius, John acquired the skills for a career in <a href="/wiki/Rhetoric" title="Rhetoric">rhetoric</a>, as well as a love of the Greek language and <a href="/wiki/Greek_literature" title="Greek literature">literature</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilken20045_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilken20045-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Eventually, he became a lawyer.
</p><p>As he grew older, however, John became more deeply committed to Christianity and went on to study <a href="/wiki/Theology" title="Theology">theology</a> under <a href="/wiki/Diodore_of_Tarsus" class="mw-redirect" title="Diodore of Tarsus">Diodore of Tarsus</a>, founder of the re-constituted <a href="/wiki/School_of_Antioch" class="mw-redirect" title="School of Antioch">School of Antioch</a>. According to the Christian historian <a href="/wiki/Sozomen" title="Sozomen">Sozomen</a>, Libanius was supposed to have said on his deathbed that John would have been his successor "if the Christians had not taken him from us".<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John lived in extreme <a href="/wiki/Asceticism" title="Asceticism">asceticism</a> and became a <a href="/wiki/Hermit" title="Hermit">hermit</a> in about 375; he spent the next two years continually standing, scarcely sleeping, and committing the <a href="/wiki/Bible" title="Bible">Bible</a> to memory. As a consequence of these practices, his stomach and kidneys were permanently damaged and poor health forced him to return to Antioch.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Diaconate_and_service_in_Antioch">Diaconate and service in Antioch</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Diaconate and service in Antioch">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Meletian_schism" class="mw-redirect" title="Meletian schism">Meletian schism</a></div>
<p>John was ordained as a <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacon</a> in 381 by the bishop <a href="/wiki/Meletius_of_Antioch" title="Meletius of Antioch">Meletius of Antioch</a> who was not then <a href="/wiki/In_communion" class="mw-redirect" title="In communion">in communion</a> with Alexandria and Rome. After the death of Meletius, John separated himself from the followers of Meletius, without joining <a href="/wiki/Paulinus_II_of_Antioch" title="Paulinus II of Antioch">Paulinus</a>, the rival of Meletius for the <a href="/wiki/Bishopric_of_Antioch" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishopric of Antioch">bishopric of Antioch</a>. But after the death of Paulinus he was ordained a <a href="/wiki/Presbyter" title="Presbyter">presbyter</a> (priest) in 386 by Flavian, the successor of Paulinus.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> He was destined later to bring about reconciliation between <a href="/wiki/Flavian_I_of_Antioch" title="Flavian I of Antioch">Flavian I of Antioch</a>, Alexandria, and Rome, thus bringing those three sees into communion for the first time in nearly seventy years.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
In Antioch, over the course of twelve years (386–397), John gained popularity because of the eloquence of his public speaking at the Golden Church, Antioch's <a href="/wiki/Cathedral" title="Cathedral">cathedral</a>, especially his insightful expositions of Bible passages and moral teaching. The most valuable of his works from this period are his <a href="/wiki/Homilies" class="mw-redirect" title="Homilies">homilies</a> on various books of the Bible. He emphasised charitable giving and was concerned with the spiritual and temporal needs of the poor. He spoke against abuse of wealth and personal property:</p><blockquote><p>Do you wish to honour the body of Christ? Do not ignore him when he is naked. Do not pay him homage in the temple clad in silk, only then to neglect him outside where he is cold and ill-clad. He who said: "This is my body" is the same who said: "You saw me hungry and you gave me no food", and "Whatever you did to the least of my brothers you did also to me"... What good is it if the <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Eucharistic</a> table is overloaded with golden chalices when your brother is dying of hunger? Start by satisfying his hunger and then with what is left you may adorn the altar as well.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>His straightforward understanding of the Scriptures – in contrast to the Alexandrian tendency towards <a href="/wiki/Allegorical_interpretation_of_the_Bible" title="Allegorical interpretation of the Bible">allegorical interpretation</a> – meant that the themes of his talks were practical, explaining the Bible's application to everyday life. Such straightforward preaching helped Chrysostom to garner popular support.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaur1910_2-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaur1910-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One incident that happened during his service in Antioch illustrates the influence of his homilies. When Chrysostom arrived in Antioch, Flavian, the bishop of the city, had to intervene with emperor <a href="/wiki/Theodosius_I" title="Theodosius I">Theodosius I</a> on behalf of citizens who had gone on a rampage mutilating statues of the emperor and his family. During the weeks of <a href="/wiki/Lent" title="Lent">Lent</a> in 387, John preached more than twenty homilies in which he entreated the people to see the error of their ways. These made a lasting impression on the general population of the city: many pagans converted to Christianity as a result of the homilies. The city was ultimately spared from severe consequences.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilken2013_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilken2013-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Archbishop_of_Constantinople">Archbishop of Constantinople</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Archbishop of Constantinople">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Origenist_Crises" class="mw-redirect" title="Origenist Crises">Origenist Crises</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Augustins_-_Saint_Jean_Chrysostome_et_l%27Imp%C3%A9ratrice_Eudoxie_-_Jean_Paul_Laurens_2004_1_156.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Augustins_-_Saint_Jean_Chrysostome_et_l%27Imp%C3%A9ratrice_Eudoxie_-_Jean_Paul_Laurens_2004_1_156.jpg/220px-Augustins_-_Saint_Jean_Chrysostome_et_l%27Imp%C3%A9ratrice_Eudoxie_-_Jean_Paul_Laurens_2004_1_156.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="172" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Augustins_-_Saint_Jean_Chrysostome_et_l%27Imp%C3%A9ratrice_Eudoxie_-_Jean_Paul_Laurens_2004_1_156.jpg/330px-Augustins_-_Saint_Jean_Chrysostome_et_l%27Imp%C3%A9ratrice_Eudoxie_-_Jean_Paul_Laurens_2004_1_156.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Augustins_-_Saint_Jean_Chrysostome_et_l%27Imp%C3%A9ratrice_Eudoxie_-_Jean_Paul_Laurens_2004_1_156.jpg/440px-Augustins_-_Saint_Jean_Chrysostome_et_l%27Imp%C3%A9ratrice_Eudoxie_-_Jean_Paul_Laurens_2004_1_156.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4973" data-file-height="3898" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Augustins_-_Saint_Jean_Chrysostome_et_l%27Imp%C3%A9ratrice_Eudoxie_-_Jean_Paul_Laurens_2004_1_156.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>John Chrysostom confronting <a href="/wiki/Aelia_Eudoxia" title="Aelia Eudoxia">Aelia Eudoxia</a>, in a 19th-century <a href="/wiki/Anti-clerical_art" title="Anti-clerical art">anti-clerical painting</a> by <a href="/wiki/Jean-Paul_Laurens" title="Jean-Paul Laurens">Jean-Paul Laurens</a></div></div></div>
<p>In the autumn of 397, John was appointed <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Constantinople" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Constantinople">archbishop of Constantinople</a>, after having been nominated without his knowledge by the <a href="/wiki/Eunuch" title="Eunuch">eunuch</a> <a href="/wiki/Eutropius_(consul_399)" title="Eutropius (consul 399)">Eutropius</a>. He had to leave Antioch in secret due to fears that the departure of such a popular figure would cause civil unrest.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his time as archbishop he adamantly refused to host lavish social gatherings, which made him popular with the common people, but unpopular with wealthy citizens and the clergy. His reforms of the clergy were also unpopular. He told visiting regional preachers to return to the churches they were meant to be serving – without any pay-out.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Also he founded a number of hospitals in Constantinople.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His time in Constantinople was more tumultuous than his time in Antioch. <a href="/wiki/Theophilus_I_of_Alexandria" title="Theophilus I of Alexandria">Theophilus</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Alexandria" title="Patriarch of Alexandria">patriarch of Alexandria</a>, wanted to bring Constantinople under his sway and opposed John's appointment to Constantinople. Theophilus had disciplined four <a href="/wiki/Roman_Egypt" title="Roman Egypt">Egyptian</a> <a href="/wiki/Monk" title="Monk">monks</a> (known as "the <a href="/wiki/Tall_Brothers" title="Tall Brothers">Tall Brothers</a>") over their support of <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>'s teachings. They fled to John and were welcomed by him. Theophilus therefore accused John of being too partial to the teaching of Origen. He made another enemy in <a href="/wiki/Aelia_Eudoxia" title="Aelia Eudoxia">Aelia Eudoxia</a>, wife of emperor <a href="/wiki/Arcadius" title="Arcadius">Arcadius</a>, who assumed that John's denunciations of extravagance in feminine dress were aimed at herself.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilken2013_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilken2013-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Eudoxia, Theophilus and other of his enemies held a <a href="/wiki/Synod" title="Synod">synod</a> in 403 (the <a href="/wiki/Synod_of_the_Oak" title="Synod of the Oak">Synod of the Oak</a>) to charge John, in which his connection to Origen was used against him. It resulted in his deposition and banishment. He was called back by Arcadius almost immediately, as the people became "tumultuous" over his departure, even threatening to burn the imperial palace.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources" title="Wikipedia:Reliable sources"><span title="The material near this tag may rely on an unreliable source. (May 2020)">unreliable source?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> There was an earthquake the night of his arrest, which Eudoxia took for a sign of God's anger, prompting her to ask Arcadius for John's reinstatement.<sup id="cite_ref-OCA_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OCA-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources" title="Wikipedia:Reliable sources"><span title="The material near this tag may rely on an unreliable source. (May 2020)">unreliable source?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Peace was short-lived. A silver statue of Eudoxia was erected in the <a href="/wiki/Augustaion" title="Augustaion">Augustaion</a>, near his cathedral, the <a href="/wiki/Constantinian_Hagia_Sophia" class="mw-redirect" title="Constantinian Hagia Sophia">Constantinian Hagia Sophia</a>. John denounced the dedication ceremonies as pagan and spoke against the empress in harsh terms: "Again <a href="/wiki/Herodias" title="Herodias">Herodias</a> raves; again she is troubled; she dances again; and again desires to receive John's head in a charger",<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources" title="Wikipedia:Reliable sources"><span title="The material near this tag may rely on an unreliable source. (May 2020)">unreliable source?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> an allusion to the events surrounding the <a href="/wiki/Death_of_John_the_Baptist" class="mw-redirect" title="Death of John the Baptist">death of John the Baptist</a>. Once again he was banished, this time to the <a href="/wiki/Caucasus" title="Caucasus">Caucasus</a> in <a href="/wiki/Abasgoi" title="Abasgoi">Abkhazia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> His banishment sparked riots among his supporters in the capital, and in the fighting the cathedral built by <a href="/wiki/Constantius_II" title="Constantius II">Constantius II</a> was burnt down, necessitating the construction of the second cathedral on the site, the <a href="/wiki/Theodosian_Hagia_Sophia" class="mw-redirect" title="Theodosian Hagia Sophia">Theodosian Hagia Sophia</a>.
</p><p>Around 405, John began to lend moral and financial support to Christian monks who were enforcing the emperors' anti-pagan laws, by destroying temples and shrines in Phoenicia and nearby regions.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Exile_and_death">Exile and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Exile and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The causes of John's exile are not clear, though Jennifer Barry suggests that they have to do with his connections to <a href="/wiki/Arianism" title="Arianism">Arianism</a>. Other historians, including <a href="/wiki/Wendy_Mayer" title="Wendy Mayer">Wendy Mayer</a> and <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Dunn" title="Geoffrey Dunn">Geoffrey Dunn</a>, have argued that "the surplus of evidence reveals a struggle between Johannite and anti-Johannite camps in Constantinople soon after John's departure and for a few years after his death".<sup id="cite_ref-barry_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-barry-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Faced with exile, John Chrysostom wrote an appeal for help to three churchmen: <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_I" title="Pope Innocent I">Pope Innocent I</a>; <a href="/wiki/Venerius_(bishop_of_Milan)" title="Venerius (bishop of Milan)">Venerius</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Milan" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Milan">bishop of Mediolanum</a> (<a href="/wiki/Milan" title="Milan">Milan</a>); and <a href="/wiki/Chromatius" title="Chromatius">Chromatius</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Aquileia" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Aquileia">bishop of Aquileia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> In 1872, church historian William Stephens wrote:
</p>
<blockquote><p>The Patriarch of the Eastern Rome appeals to the great bishops of the West, as the champions of an ecclesiastical discipline which he confesses himself unable to enforce, or to see any prospect of establishing. No jealousy is entertained of the Patriarch of the Old Rome by the patriarch of the New Rome. The interference of Innocent is courted, a certain primacy is accorded him, but at the same time he is not addressed as a supreme arbitrator; assistance and sympathy are solicited from him as from an elder brother, and two other prelates of Italy are joint recipients with him of the appeal.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Pope Innocent I protested John's banishment from Constantinople to the town of Cucusus (<a href="/wiki/G%C3%B6ksun" title="Göksun">Göksun</a>) in <a href="/wiki/Cappadocia" title="Cappadocia">Cappadocia</a>, but to no avail. Innocent sent a delegation to intercede on behalf of John in 405. It was led by <a href="/wiki/Gaudentius_of_Brescia" title="Gaudentius of Brescia">Gaudentius of Brescia</a>; Gaudentius and his companions, two bishops, encountered many difficulties and never reached their goal of entering Constantinople.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John wrote letters which still held great influence in Constantinople. As a result of this, he was further exiled from Cucusus (where he stayed from 404 to 407) to <a href="/wiki/Pitiunt" class="mw-redirect" title="Pitiunt">Pitiunt</a> (Pityus) (in modern <a href="/wiki/Georgia_(country)" title="Georgia (country)">Georgia</a>) where his tomb is a <a href="/wiki/Shrine" title="Shrine">shrine</a> for <a href="/wiki/Pilgrim" title="Pilgrim">pilgrims</a>. He never reached this destination, as he died at <a href="/wiki/Comana_Pontica" title="Comana Pontica">Comana Pontica</a> on 14 September 407 during the journey.<sup id="cite_ref-OCA_31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OCA-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
He died in the Presbyterium or community of the clergy belonging to the church of <a href="/wiki/Basiliscus_of_Comana" title="Basiliscus of Comana">Saint Basiliscus of Comana</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEButler1821297_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEButler1821297-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
His last words are said to have been "<span title="Greek-language text"><span lang="el">Δόξα τῷ Θεῷ πάντων ἕνεκεν</span></span>" ('Glory be to God for all things').<sup id="cite_ref-OCA_31-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OCA-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration_and_canonization">Veneration and canonization</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Veneration and canonization">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Chrysostom_Louvre_OA3970.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/John_Chrysostom_Louvre_OA3970.jpg/220px-John_Chrysostom_Louvre_OA3970.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="323" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/John_Chrysostom_Louvre_OA3970.jpg/330px-John_Chrysostom_Louvre_OA3970.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/John_Chrysostom_Louvre_OA3970.jpg/440px-John_Chrysostom_Louvre_OA3970.jpg 2x" data-file-width="837" data-file-height="1227" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Chrysostom_Louvre_OA3970.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Byzantine" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine">Byzantine</a> 11th-century <a href="/wiki/Soapstone" title="Soapstone">soapstone</a> <a href="/wiki/Relief" title="Relief">relief</a> of John Chrysostom, <a href="/wiki/Louvre" title="Louvre">Louvre</a></div></div></div>
<p>John came to be venerated as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> soon after his death. Almost immediately after, an anonymous supporter of John (known as pseudo-Martyrius) wrote a funeral oration to reclaim John as a symbol of Christian <a href="/wiki/Orthodoxy" title="Orthodoxy">orthodoxy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-barry_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-barry-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> But three decades later, some of his adherents in Constantinople remained in <a href="/wiki/Schism_in_Christianity" title="Schism in Christianity">schism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Proclus_of_Constantinople" title="Proclus of Constantinople">Proclus</a>, archbishop of Constantinople (434–446), hoping to bring about the reconciliation of the Johannites, preached a homily praising his predecessor in the Church of <a href="/wiki/Hagia_Sophia" title="Hagia Sophia">Hagia Sophia</a>. He said, "O John, your life was filled with sorrow, but your death was glorious. Your grave is blessed and reward is great, by the grace and mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ O graced one, having conquered the bounds of time and place! Love has conquered space, unforgetting memory has annihilated the limits, and place does not hinder the <a href="/wiki/Miracle" title="Miracle">miracles</a> of the saint."<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>These homilies helped to mobilize public opinion, and the patriarch received permission from the emperor to return Chrysostom's <a href="/wiki/Relics" class="mw-redirect" title="Relics">relics</a> to Constantinople, where they were enshrined in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Holy_Apostles" title="Church of the Holy Apostles">Church of the Holy Apostles</a> on 28 January 438. The <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> commemorates him as a "Great Ecumenical Teacher", with <a href="/wiki/Basil_the_Great" class="mw-redirect" title="Basil the Great">Basil the Great</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gregory_the_Theologian" class="mw-redirect" title="Gregory the Theologian">Gregory the Theologian</a>. These three saints, in addition to having their own individual commemorations throughout the year, are commemorated together on 30 January, a feast known as the <a href="/wiki/Synaxis" title="Synaxis">Synaxis</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Three_Hierarchs" class="mw-redirect" title="Three Hierarchs">Three Hierarchs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the Eastern Orthodox Church there are several <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast days</a> dedicated to him:
</p>
<ul><li>27 January, Translation of the relics of Saint John Chrysostom from Comana to Constantinople<sup id="cite_ref-calendar_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-calendar-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>30 January, Synaxis of the Three Great Hierarchs<sup id="cite_ref-calendar_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-calendar-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>14 September, Repose of Saint John Chrysostom<sup id="cite_ref-calendar_45-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-calendar-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>13 November, celebration was transferred from 14 September by the 10th century AD as the <a href="/wiki/Exaltation_of_the_Holy_Cross" class="mw-redirect" title="Exaltation of the Holy Cross">Exaltation of the Holy Cross</a> became more prominent.<sup id="cite_ref-calendar_45-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-calendar-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> According to Brian Croke, 13 November is the date news of John Chrysostom's death reached Constantinople.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>In 1908 <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_X" title="Pope Pius X">Pope Pius X</a> named him the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of preachers.<sup id="cite_ref-episcopal_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-episcopal-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Some 700 sermons and 246 letters by John Chrysostom survive, plus biblical commentaries, moral discourses, and theological treatises.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Homilies">Homilies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Homilies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Paschal_Homily">Paschal Homily</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Paschal Homily">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicephorus_III_and_chrysostome_BnF_Coislin79_fol2v.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Nicephorus_III_and_chrysostome_BnF_Coislin79_fol2v.jpg/220px-Nicephorus_III_and_chrysostome_BnF_Coislin79_fol2v.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="277" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Nicephorus_III_and_chrysostome_BnF_Coislin79_fol2v.jpg/330px-Nicephorus_III_and_chrysostome_BnF_Coislin79_fol2v.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/c7/Nicephorus_III_and_chrysostome_BnF_Coislin79_fol2v.jpg 2x" data-file-width="381" data-file-height="480" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicephorus_III_and_chrysostome_BnF_Coislin79_fol2v.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The Byzantine emperor <a href="/wiki/Nicephorus_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicephorus III">Nicephorus III</a> receives a book of homilies from John Chrysostom; the <a href="/wiki/Archangel_Michael" class="mw-redirect" title="Archangel Michael">Archangel Michael</a> stands on his left (11th-century illuminated <a href="/wiki/Manuscript" title="Manuscript">manuscript</a>).</div></div></div>
<p>The best known of his many homilies is an extremely brief one, the <a href="/wiki/Paschal_Homily" title="Paschal Homily">Paschal Homily</a> (<i>Hieratikon</i>), which is read at the first service of <a href="/wiki/Easter#Eastern_Christianity_2" title="Easter">Pascha (Easter)</a>, the midnight <a href="/wiki/Orthros" title="Orthros">Orthros</a> (<a href="/wiki/Matins#Byzantine_Rite" title="Matins">Matins</a>), in the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="General">General</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: General">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Chrysostom's extant homiletical works are vast, including many hundreds of exegetical homilies on both the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> (especially the works of <a href="/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle" title="Paul the Apostle">Paul the Apostle</a>) and the <a href="/wiki/Old_Testament" title="Old Testament">Old Testament</a> (particularly on <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Genesis" title="Book of Genesis">Genesis</a>). Among his extant exegetical works are sixty-seven homilies on Genesis, fifty-nine on the <a href="/wiki/Psalms" title="Psalms">Psalms</a>, ninety on the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Gospel of Matthew</a>, eighty-eight on the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_John" title="Gospel of John">Gospel of John</a>, and fifty-five on the <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles" title="Acts of the Apostles">Acts of the Apostles</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaur1910_2-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaur1910-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The homilies were written down by stenographers and subsequently circulated, revealing a style that tended to be direct and greatly personal, but formed by the rhetorical conventions of his time and place.<sup id="cite_ref-lewy_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lewy-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> In general, his homiletical theology displays much characteristic of the <a href="/wiki/Antiochian_school" class="mw-redirect" title="Antiochian school">Antiochian school</a> (i.e., somewhat more literal in interpreting biblical events), but he also uses a good deal of the allegorical interpretation more associated with the <a href="/wiki/Alexandrian_school" title="Alexandrian school">Alexandrian school</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaur1910_2-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaur1910-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
John's social and religious world was formed by the continuing and pervasive presence of paganism in the life of the city. One of his regular topics was the paganism in the culture of Constantinople, and in his homilies he thunders against popular pagan amusements: the <a href="/wiki/Theatre_of_ancient_Greece" title="Theatre of ancient Greece">theatre</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chariot_racing" title="Chariot racing">horseraces</a>, and the revelry surrounding holidays.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilken200430_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilken200430-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> In particular, he criticizes Christians for taking part in such activities: </p><blockquote><p>If you ask [Christians] who is <a href="/wiki/Amos_(prophet)" title="Amos (prophet)">Amos</a> or <a href="/wiki/Obadiah" title="Obadiah">Obadiah</a>, how many apostles there were or prophets, they stand mute; but if you ask them about the horses or drivers, they answer with more solemnity than <a href="/wiki/Sophism" class="mw-redirect" title="Sophism">sophists</a> or rhetors.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilken200430_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilken200430-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p>
One of the recurring features of John's homilies is his emphasis on care for the needy.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Echoing themes found in the Gospel of Matthew, he calls upon the rich to lay aside materialism in favor of helping the poor, often employing all of his rhetorical skills to shame wealthy people to abandon <a href="/wiki/Conspicuous_consumption" title="Conspicuous consumption">conspicuous consumption</a>: </p><blockquote><p>Do you pay such honor to your excrements as to receive them into a silver chamber-pot when another man made in the image of God is perishing in the cold?<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p> Along these lines, he wrote often about the need for <a href="/wiki/Alms" title="Alms">almsgiving</a> and its importance alongside fasting and prayer, e.g. "Prayer without almsgiving is unfruitful."<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Hosios_Loukas_(nave,_south_east_conch)_-_John_Chrysostom_-_detail.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Hosios_Loukas_%28nave%2C_south_east_conch%29_-_John_Chrysostom_-_detail.jpg/220px-Hosios_Loukas_%28nave%2C_south_east_conch%29_-_John_Chrysostom_-_detail.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="253" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Hosios_Loukas_%28nave%2C_south_east_conch%29_-_John_Chrysostom_-_detail.jpg/330px-Hosios_Loukas_%28nave%2C_south_east_conch%29_-_John_Chrysostom_-_detail.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Hosios_Loukas_%28nave%2C_south_east_conch%29_-_John_Chrysostom_-_detail.jpg/440px-Hosios_Loukas_%28nave%2C_south_east_conch%29_-_John_Chrysostom_-_detail.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1297" data-file-height="1493" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Hosios_Loukas_(nave,_south_east_conch)_-_John_Chrysostom_-_detail.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>11th-century <a href="/wiki/Conch_(architecture)" class="mw-redirect" title="Conch (architecture)">conch</a> mosaic of John Chrysostom from the south-east apse of the <a href="/wiki/Nave" title="Nave">nave</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Hosios_Loukas" title="Hosios Loukas">Hosios Loukas</a> monastery</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Cyril_of_Alexandria" title="Cyril of Alexandria">Cyril of Alexandria</a> attributed the destruction of the <a href="/wiki/Ephesus" title="Ephesus">Ephesian</a> <a href="/wiki/Temple_of_Artemis" title="Temple of Artemis">Temple of Artemis</a> to John Chrysostom, referring to him as "the destroyer of the demons and overthrower of the temple of Diana". A later Archbishop of Constantinople, <a href="/wiki/Proclus_of_Constantinople" title="Proclus of Constantinople">Proclus</a> repeated the allegation, saying "In Ephesus, he despoiled the art of Midas". Both claims are considered spurious.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Homilies_against_Jews_and_Judaizing_Christians">Homilies against Jews and Judaizing Christians</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Homilies against Jews and Judaizing Christians">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Adversus_Judaeos" title="Adversus Judaeos">Adversus Judaeos</a></div>
<p>During his first two years as a presbyter in Antioch (386–387), John denounced <a href="/wiki/Jew" class="mw-redirect" title="Jew">Jews</a> and <a href="/wiki/Judaizers" title="Judaizers">Judaizing Christians</a> in a series of eight homilies delivered to Christians in his congregation who were taking part in <a href="/wiki/Jewish_festivals" class="mw-redirect" title="Jewish festivals">Jewish festivals</a> and other Jewish observances.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilken2004xv_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilken2004xv-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> It is disputed whether the main targets were specifically Judaizers or Jews in general. His homilies were expressed in the conventional manner, utilizing the uncompromising <a href="/wiki/Rhetoric" title="Rhetoric">rhetorical</a> form known as the <i>psogos</i> (Greek: blame, censure).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilken2004xv_54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilken2004xv-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One of the purposes of these homilies was to prevent Christians from participating in Jewish customs, and thus prevent the perceived erosion of Chrysostom's flock. In his homilies, John criticized those "Judaizing Christians", who were participating in Jewish festivals and taking part in other Jewish observances, such as the <a href="/wiki/Shabbat" title="Shabbat">shabbat</a>, submitted to <a href="/wiki/Circumcision_in_the_Bible" class="mw-redirect" title="Circumcision in the Bible">circumcision</a> and made pilgrimage to Jewish holy places.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilken2004xv_54-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilken2004xv-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> There had been a revival of Jewish faith and tolerance in Antioch in 361, so Chrysostom's followers and the greater Christian community were in contact with Jews frequently, and Chrysostom was concerned that this interaction would draw Christians away from their faith identity.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John claimed that <a href="/wiki/Synagogue" title="Synagogue">synagogues</a> were full of Christians, especially Christian women, on the shabbats and Jewish festivals, because they loved the solemnity of the Jewish liturgy and enjoyed listening to the <a href="/wiki/Shofar" title="Shofar">shofar</a> on <a href="/wiki/Rosh_Hashanah" title="Rosh Hashanah">Rosh Hashanah</a>, and applauded famous preachers in accordance with the contemporary custom.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Due to Chrysostom's stature in the Christian church, both locally and within the greater church hierarchy, his sermons were fairly successful in spreading anti-Jewish sentiment. This prompted the introduction of anti-Jewish legislation and social regulations, increasing the separation between the two communities.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In Greek the homilies are called <i>Kata Ioudaiōn</i> (<span title="Greek-language text"><span lang="el">Κατὰ Ἰουδαίων</span></span>), which is translated as <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Adversus Judaeos</i></span> in Latin and 'Against the Jews' in English.<sup id="cite_ref-trans_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-trans-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> The original <a href="/wiki/Benedictine" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedictine">Benedictine</a> editor of the homilies, <a href="/wiki/Bernard_de_Montfaucon" title="Bernard de Montfaucon">Bernard de Montfaucon</a>, gives the following footnote to the title: "A discourse against the Jews; but it was delivered against those who were Judaizing and keeping the fasts with them [the Jews]."<sup id="cite_ref-trans_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-trans-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Patristics" title="Patristics">Patristics</a> scholars, opposition to any particular view during the late 4th century was conventionally expressed in a manner, utilizing the rhetorical form known as the psogos, whose literary conventions were to vilify opponents in an uncompromising manner; thus, it has been argued that to call Chrysostom an "anti-Semite" is to employ anachronistic terminology in a way incongruous with historical context and record.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilken2004124–126_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilken2004124–126-59">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> This does not preclude assertions that Chrysostom's theology was a form of anti-Jewish <a href="/wiki/Supersessionism" title="Supersessionism">supersessionism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Anglican priest <a href="/wiki/James_Parkes_(clergyman)" class="mw-redirect" title="James Parkes (clergyman)">James Parkes</a> called Chrysostom's writing on Jews "the most horrible and violent denunciations of Judaism to be found in the writings of a Christian theologian".<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> According to historian <a href="/wiki/William_I._Brustein" title="William I. Brustein">William I. Brustein</a>, his sermons against Jews gave further momentum to the idea that Jews are <a href="/wiki/Jewish_deicide" title="Jewish deicide">collectively responsible for the death of Jesus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Steven_T._Katz" title="Steven T. Katz">Steven T. Katz</a> cites Chrysostom's homilies as "the decisive turn in the history of Christian <a href="/wiki/Anti-Judaism" title="Anti-Judaism">anti-Judaism</a>, a turn whose ultimate disfiguring consequence was enacted in the political antisemitism of Adolf Hitler."<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Three_Holy_Hierarchs_(Novgorod).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Three_Holy_Hierarchs_%28Novgorod%29.jpg/220px-Three_Holy_Hierarchs_%28Novgorod%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="274" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Three_Holy_Hierarchs_%28Novgorod%29.jpg/330px-Three_Holy_Hierarchs_%28Novgorod%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Three_Holy_Hierarchs_%28Novgorod%29.jpg/440px-Three_Holy_Hierarchs_%28Novgorod%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2359" data-file-height="2937" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Three_Holy_Hierarchs_(Novgorod).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>John Chrysostom with <a href="/wiki/Basil_of_Caesarea" title="Basil of Caesarea">Basil of Caesarea</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gregory_of_Nazianzus" title="Gregory of Nazianzus">Gregory of Nazianzus</a> on a late-15th-century icon of the <a href="/wiki/Three_Holy_Hierarchs" title="Three Holy Hierarchs">Three Holy Hierarchs</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_of_St_Sophia,_Novgorod" class="mw-redirect" title="Cathedral of St Sophia, Novgorod">Cathedral of St Sophia, Novgorod</a></div></div></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Homily_against_homosexuality">Homily against homosexuality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Homily against homosexuality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4><p>
According to Robert H. Allen, "Chrysostom's learning and eloquence spans and sums up a long age of ever-growing moral outrage, fear and loathing of homosexuality."<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> His most notable discourse in this regard is his fourth homily on <a href="/wiki/Romans_1:26" class="mw-redirect" title="Romans 1:26">Romans 1:26</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> where he argues as follows: </p><blockquote><p>All these affections then were vile, but chiefly the mad lust after males; for the soul is more the sufferer in sins, and more dishonored, than the body in diseases. ... [The men] have done an insult to nature itself. And a yet more disgraceful thing than these is it, when even the women seek after these intercourses, who ought to have more sense of shame than men.<sup id="cite_ref-Homilies_on_Romans_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Homilies_on_Romans-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>He says the active male victimizes the passive male in a way that leaves him more enduringly dishonored than even a victim of murder since the victim of this act must "live under" the shame of the "insolency".<sup id="cite_ref-Homilies_on_Romans_66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Homilies_on_Romans-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> The victim of a murder, by contrast, carries no dishonor. He asserts that punishment will be found in <a href="/wiki/Hell" title="Hell">Hell</a> for such transgressors and that women can be guilty of the sin as much as men. Chrysostom argues that the male passive partner has effectively renounced his manhood and become a woman – such an individual deserves to be "driven out and stoned". He attributes the cause to "luxury". "Do not, he means (Paul), because you have heard that they burned, suppose that the evil was only in desire. For the greater part of it came of their luxuriousness, which also kindled into flame their lust".<sup id="cite_ref-Homilies_on_Romans_66-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Homilies_on_Romans-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to scholar Michael Carden, Chrysostom was particularly influential in shaping early Christian thought that same-sex desire was an evil, claiming that he altered a traditional interpretation of <a href="/wiki/Sodom_and_Gomorrah" title="Sodom and Gomorrah">Sodom</a> as a place of inhospitality to one where the sexual transgressions of the Sodomites became paramount.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> However, other scholars – such as Kruger<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> and Nortjé-Meyer<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> – dispute this, arguing that the author of the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Jude" title="Epistle of Jude">Epistle of Jude</a> already interpreted the sin of Sodom as homosexuality in the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Treatises">Treatises</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Treatises">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Apart from his homilies, a number of John's other treatises have had a lasting influence. One such work is John's early treatise <i>Against Those Who Oppose the Monastic Life</i>, written while he was a deacon (sometime before 386), which was directed to parents, pagan as well as Christian, whose sons were contemplating a monastic vocation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilken200426_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilken200426-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> Chrysostom wrote that, already in his day, it was customary for Antiochenes to send their sons to be educated by monks.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Another important treatise written by John is titled <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=On_the_Priesthood&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="On the Priesthood (page does not exist)">On the Priesthood</a></i> (written 390/391, it contains in Book 1 an account of his early years and a defence of his flight from ordination by bishop Meletios of Antioch, and then proceeds in later books to expound on his exalted understanding of the priesthood). Two other notable books by John are <i>Instructions to Catechumens</i> and <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=On_the_Incomprehensibility_of_the_Divine_Nature&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="On the Incomprehensibility of the Divine Nature (page does not exist)">On the Incomprehensibility of the Divine Nature</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> In addition, he wrote a series of letters to the <a href="/wiki/Deaconess" title="Deaconess">deaconess</a> <a href="/wiki/St._Olympias" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Olympias">Olympias</a>, of which seventeen are extant.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Liturgy">Liturgy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Liturgy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Beyond his preaching, the other lasting legacy of John is his influence on Christian liturgy. Two of his writings are particularly notable. He harmonized the liturgical life of the church by revising the prayers and rubrics of the <a href="/wiki/Divine_Liturgy" title="Divine Liturgy">Divine Liturgy</a>, or celebration of the Holy Eucharist. To this day, Eastern Orthodox and Eastern Catholic Churches of the Byzantine Rite typically celebrate the <i><a href="/wiki/Divine_Liturgy_of_Saint_John_Chrysostom" class="mw-redirect" title="Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom">Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom</a></i> as the normal Eucharistic liturgy, although his exact connection with it remains a matter of debate among experts.<sup id="cite_ref-Parry_2001,_pp._268–269_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Parry_2001,_pp._268–269-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_and_influence">Legacy and influence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Legacy and influence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:St.Patrick%27s_Cathedral_NYC2.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/St.Patrick%27s_Cathedral_NYC2.jpg/220px-St.Patrick%27s_Cathedral_NYC2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="331" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/St.Patrick%27s_Cathedral_NYC2.jpg/330px-St.Patrick%27s_Cathedral_NYC2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/St.Patrick%27s_Cathedral_NYC2.jpg/440px-St.Patrick%27s_Cathedral_NYC2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="3008" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:St.Patrick%27s_Cathedral_NYC2.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A sculpture of John Chrysostom in <a href="/wiki/St._Patrick%27s_Cathedral,_New_York" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Patrick&#39;s Cathedral, New York">Saint Patrick's Cathedral</a>, New York City</div></div></div>
<p>During a time when city clergy were subject to criticism for their high lifestyle, John was determined to reform his clergy in Constantinople. These efforts were met with resistance and limited success. He was an excellent preacher<sup id="cite_ref-Parry_2001,_pp._268–269_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Parry_2001,_pp._268–269-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> whose homilies and writings are still studied and quoted. As a theologian, he has been and continues to be very important in <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christianity" title="Eastern Christianity">Eastern Christianity</a>, and is generally considered among the Three Holy Hierarchs of the Greek Church, but has been less important to Western Christianity. His writings have survived to the present day more so than any of the other <a href="/wiki/Greek_Fathers" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek Fathers">Greek Fathers</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaur1910_2-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaur1910-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Influence_on_the_Catechism_of_the_Catholic_Church_and_clergy">Influence on the Catechism of the Catholic Church and clergy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Influence on the Catechism of the Catholic Church and clergy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3><p>
Regardless of his lesser influence compared to, say, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a>, John's influence on church teachings is interwoven throughout the current <a href="/wiki/Catechism_of_the_Catholic_Church" title="Catechism of the Catholic Church">Catechism of the Catholic Church</a> (revised 1992).<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The Catechism cites him in eighteen sections, particularly his reflections on the purpose of prayer and the meaning of the <a href="/wiki/Lord%27s_Prayer" title="Lord&#39;s Prayer">Lord's Prayer</a>:<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> </p><blockquote><p>Consider how [Jesus Christ] teaches us to be humble, by making us see that our virtue does not depend on our work alone but on grace from on high. He commands each of the faithful who prays to do so universally, for the whole world. For he did not say "thy will be done in me or in us", but "on earth", the whole earth, so that error may be banished from it, truth take root in it, all vice be destroyed on it, virtue flourish on it, and earth no longer differ from heaven.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Christian clerics, such as R. S. Storr, refer to him as "one of the most eloquent preachers who ever since apostolic times have brought to men the divine tidings of truth and love",<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Inline_citation#When_you_must_use_inline_citations" title="Wikipedia:Inline citation"><span title="The text near this tag needs a citation. (October 2022)">This quote needs a citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> and the 19th-century <a href="/wiki/John_Henry_Newman" title="John Henry Newman">John Henry Newman</a> described John as a "bright, cheerful, gentle soul; a sensitive heart".<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Music_and_literature">Music and literature</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Music and literature">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>John's liturgical legacy has inspired several musical compositions. Particularly noteworthy<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> are <a href="/wiki/Sergei_Rachmaninoff" title="Sergei Rachmaninoff">Sergei Rachmaninoff</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Liturgy_of_St._John_Chrysostom_(Rachmaninoff)" title="Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom (Rachmaninoff)">Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom</a></i>, Op. 31, composed in 1910,<sup id="cite_ref-hyperion_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hyperion-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> one of his two major unaccompanied choral works; <a href="/wiki/Pyotr_Tchaikovsky" class="mw-redirect" title="Pyotr Tchaikovsky">Pyotr Tchaikovsky</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Liturgy_of_St._John_Chrysostom_(Tchaikovsky)" title="Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom (Tchaikovsky)">Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom</a></i>, Op. 41; and Ukrainian composer <a href="/wiki/Kyrylo_Stetsenko" title="Kyrylo Stetsenko">Kyrylo Stetsenko</a>'s <i>Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom</i>. <a href="/wiki/Arvo_P%C3%A4rt" title="Arvo Pärt">Arvo Pärt</a>'s <i>Litany</i> sets Chrysostom's twenty-four prayers, one for each hour of the day,<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> for soli, mixed choir and orchestra. And the compositions of <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Grechaninov" class="mw-redirect" title="Alexander Grechaninov">Alexander Grechaninovs</a> <i>Liturgy of Johannes Chrysostomos</i> No. 1, Op. 13 (1897), <i>Liturgy of Johannes Chrysostomos</i> No. 2, Op. 29 (1902), <i>Liturgia Domestica (Liturgy Johannes Chrysostomos</i> No. 3), Op. 79 (1917) and <i>Liturgy of Johannes Chrysostomos</i> No. 4, Op. 177 (1943) are noteworthy.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/James_Joyce" title="James Joyce">James Joyce</a>'s novel <i><a href="/wiki/Ulysses_(novel)" title="Ulysses (novel)">Ulysses</a></i> includes a character named Mulligan who brings 'Chrysostomos' into another character (Stephen Dedalus)'s mind because Mulligan's gold-stopped teeth and his gift of the gab earn him the title which St. John Chrysostom's preaching earned him, 'golden-mouthed':<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> "[Mulligan] peered sideways up and gave a long low whistle of call, then paused awhile in rapt attention, his even white teeth glistening here and there with gold points. Chrysostomos."<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Legend_of_the_penance_of_Saint_John_Chrysostom">Legend of the penance of Saint John Chrysostom</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Legend of the penance of Saint John Chrysostom">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Lucas_Cranach_the_Elder_-_The_Penance_of_St._John_Chrysostom.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Lucas_Cranach_the_Elder_-_The_Penance_of_St._John_Chrysostom.jpg/220px-Lucas_Cranach_the_Elder_-_The_Penance_of_St._John_Chrysostom.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="281" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Lucas_Cranach_the_Elder_-_The_Penance_of_St._John_Chrysostom.jpg/330px-Lucas_Cranach_the_Elder_-_The_Penance_of_St._John_Chrysostom.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Lucas_Cranach_the_Elder_-_The_Penance_of_St._John_Chrysostom.jpg/440px-Lucas_Cranach_the_Elder_-_The_Penance_of_St._John_Chrysostom.jpg 2x" data-file-width="470" data-file-height="600" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Lucas_Cranach_the_Elder_-_The_Penance_of_St._John_Chrysostom.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>The Penance of St. John Chrysostom</i>. Engraving by <a href="/wiki/Lucas_Cranach_the_Elder" title="Lucas Cranach the Elder">Lucas Cranach the Elder</a>, 1509. The saint can be seen in the background on all fours, whilst the princess and their baby dominate the foreground.</div></div></div>
<p>A late medieval legend relates that, when John Chrysostom was a <a href="/wiki/Hermit" title="Hermit">hermit</a> in the desert, he was approached by a royal princess in distress.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup><sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> John, thinking she was a demon, at first refused to help her, but the princess convinced him that she was a Christian and would be devoured by wild beasts if she were not allowed to enter his cave. He therefore admitted her, carefully dividing the cave in two parts, one for each of them. In spite of these precautions, the sin of <a href="/wiki/Fornication" title="Fornication">fornication</a> was committed, and in an attempt to hide it the distraught John took the princess and threw her over a precipice. He then went to Rome to beg absolution, which was refused. Realising the appalling nature of his crimes, Chrysostom made a vow that he would never rise from the ground until his sins were expiated, and for years he lived like a beast, crawling on all fours and feeding on wild grasses and roots. Subsequently, the princess reappeared, alive, and suckling John's baby, who miraculously pronounced his sins forgiven. This last scene was very popular from the late 15th century onwards as a subject for engravers and artists. The theme was depicted by <a href="/wiki/Albrecht_D%C3%BCrer" title="Albrecht Dürer">Albrecht Dürer</a> around 1496,<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Hans_Sebald_Beham" class="mw-redirect" title="Hans Sebald Beham">Hans Sebald Beham</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lucas_Cranach_the_Elder" title="Lucas Cranach the Elder">Lucas Cranach the Elder</a>, among others. <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a> mocked this same legend in his <i><a href="/wiki/Die_L%C3%BCgend_von_S._Johanne_Chrysostomo" title="Die Lügend von S. Johanne Chrysostomo">Die Lügend von S. Johanne Chrysostomo</a></i> (1537) to analyse the pitfalls of the Christian <a href="/wiki/Legendary_(hagiography)" title="Legendary (hagiography)">Legendary (hagiography)</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> The legend was recorded in <a href="/wiki/Croatia" title="Croatia">Croatia</a> in the 16th century.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:No_original_research#Primary,_secondary_and_tertiary_sources" title="Wikipedia:No original research"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable secondary sources. (May 2020)">non-primary source needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relics">Relics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Relics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Menologion_of_Basil_061.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Menologion_of_Basil_061.jpg/220px-Menologion_of_Basil_061.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Menologion_of_Basil_061.jpg/330px-Menologion_of_Basil_061.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Menologion_of_Basil_061.jpg/440px-Menologion_of_Basil_061.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1772" data-file-height="1178" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Menologion_of_Basil_061.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The return of the relics of Saint John Chrysostom to the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Holy_Apostles" title="Church of the Holy Apostles">Church of the Holy Apostles</a> in <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a></div></div></div>
<p>John Chrysostom died in the city of <a href="/wiki/Comana_Pontica" title="Comana Pontica">Comana</a> in 407 on his way to his place of exile. There his <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a> remained until 438 when, thirty years after his death, they were transferred to <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a> during the reign of the empress <a href="/wiki/Aelia_Eudoxia" title="Aelia Eudoxia">Eudoxia</a>'s son, the emperor <a href="/wiki/Theodosius_II" title="Theodosius II">Theodosius II</a> (408–450), under the guidance of John's disciple, <a href="/wiki/Proclus_of_Constantinople" title="Proclus of Constantinople">Proclus</a>, who by that time had become archbishop of Constantinople (434–447).<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Most of John's relics were looted from Constantinople by <a href="/wiki/Fourth_Crusade" title="Fourth Crusade">crusaders</a> in 1204 and taken to Rome, but some of his bones were returned to the Orthodox Church on 27 November 2004 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Since 2004 the relics have been enshrined in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_St._George,_Istanbul" class="mw-redirect" title="Church of St. George, Istanbul">Church of St. George, Istanbul</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The skull, however, having been kept at the monastery at <a href="/wiki/Vatopedi" title="Vatopedi">Vatopedi</a> on <a href="/wiki/Mount_Athos" title="Mount Athos">Mount Athos</a> in northern Greece, was not among the relics that were taken by the crusaders in the 13th century. In 1655, at the request of <a href="/wiki/Alexis_of_Russia" title="Alexis of Russia">Tsar Alexei Mikhailovich</a>, the skull was taken to Russia, for which the monastery was compensated in the sum of 2,000 rubles. In 1693, having received a request from the Vatopedi Monastery for the return of Saint John's skull, <a href="/wiki/Peter_I_of_Russia" class="mw-redirect" title="Peter I of Russia">Tsar Peter the Great</a> ordered that the skull remain in Russia but that the monastery was to be paid 500 rubles every four years. The Russian state archives document these payments up until 1735. The skull was kept at the <a href="/wiki/Moscow_Kremlin" class="mw-redirect" title="Moscow Kremlin">Moscow Kremlin</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_of_the_Dormition" class="mw-redirect" title="Cathedral of the Dormition">Cathedral of the Dormition of the Mother of God</a>, until 1920, when it was confiscated by the <a href="/wiki/Soviets" class="mw-redirect" title="Soviets">Soviets</a> and placed in the Museum of Silver Antiquities. In 1988, in connection with the 1,000th anniversary of the <a href="/wiki/Baptism_of_Russia" class="mw-redirect" title="Baptism of Russia">Baptism of Russia</a>, the head, along with other important relics, was returned to the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church" title="Russian Orthodox Church">Russian Orthodox Church</a> and kept at the <a href="/wiki/Epiphany_Cathedral_at_Yelokhovo" class="mw-redirect" title="Epiphany Cathedral at Yelokhovo">Epiphany Cathedral</a>, until being moved to the <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_of_Christ_the_Saviour" title="Cathedral of Christ the Saviour">Cathedral of Christ the Saviour</a> after its restoration.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2015)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Today, the monastery at Vatopedi posits a rival claim to possessing the skull of John Chrysostom, and there a skull is venerated by pilgrims to the monastery as that of Saint John. Two sites in Italy also claim to have the saint's skull: the <a href="/wiki/Florence_Cathedral" title="Florence Cathedral">Basilica di Santa Maria del Fiore</a> in Florence and the <i><a href="/wiki/Camposanto_Monumentale" class="mw-redirect" title="Camposanto Monumentale">Dal Pozzo</a></i> chapel in Pisa. The right hand of Saint John<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources" title="Wikipedia:Reliable sources"><span title="The material near this tag may rely on an unreliable source. (May 2020)">unreliable source?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> is preserved on Mount Athos, and numerous smaller relics are scattered throughout the world.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Collected_works">Collected works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Chrysostom&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Collected works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
